What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I guess what brought me in is this thing that keeps happening with food, mostly late at night. It sounds kind of simple when I say it, but it doesn’t feel simple when it’s happening.\n\nI’ll be in the kitchen, maybe just picking at something, and I tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago, stop there.” Like I know I should stop. I’m not confused about it. But then my hand just keeps going back for more, like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore. That part worries me the most, honestly. It’s not just being hungry or wanting a snack. It feels like once I start, stopping is... I don’t know, it’s like I lose the say in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say ashamed every time. I mean… sometimes I just feel kind of bad about it, like disappointed in myself, but I don’t always sit there naming it as guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nAfterward I’ll look at what I ate or just notice the kitchen and think, “Why did I do that?” But other times I’m more tired than anything, like I just want to go to bed and not think about it. So… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to answer. There’s definitely not a good feeling after, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt or shame exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. I think that’s probably what I’m talking about, even though in the moment I don’t always call it that.\n\nLike some nights after work, I’ll come home and I’ll start with something that seems normal, like one tamale or a little bit of leftovers. And then it turns into several tamales, and then maybe there’s pozole in the fridge, so I’ll heat up a big bowl of that, and then I’m grabbing tortillas with it, and then if there’s rice or beans left I’ll start eating those too.\n\nAnd the weird part is I’ll be standing there in the kitchen acting like I’m just cleaning up leftovers or putting things away, like it’s not really a meal, but when I look at it all together… yeah, it’s a lot of food, and it happens pretty fast. It’s not like I sat down and planned to eat all that. It just kind of keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to get rid of the calories from these late-night eating episodes, like by vomiting, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve never made myself throw up or anything like that. And laxatives, no, not for that. I wouldn’t even really know how people do that, honestly.\n\nWith exercise… I mean, I sometimes tell myself the next day, “Okay, you should move around more,” or I’ll feel like I should be more careful, but it’s not like I’m going out at midnight to run for two hours or punishing myself at the gym. It’s more just that thought in my head, like I should make up for it somehow, but I don’t really do some specific thing to get rid of it.\n\nMostly after it happens, I just feel heavy and tired, and I kind of want to not deal with it. I’ll clean up, or not even fully clean up sometimes, and go to bed. So… no, I don’t think I’m doing anything like vomiting or laxatives or extreme exercise.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’d say… for the past several months it’s been most weeks. Not every single night or anything like that, but usually at least once in a week, sometimes I realize after the fact like, “Oh, that was one of those nights again.”\n\nIt tends to be on nights when there’s a lot of food already made, like leftovers in the fridge, and everybody else has gone to bed. That’s when it’s easier for it to happen, I guess, because I’m downstairs or in the kitchen by myself and there’s all this food there. If there aren’t many leftovers, or if people are still up, it’s less likely to turn into that. But yeah… most weeks, at least once, for a few months now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me the most, I think. Because it’s not just, “Oh, I decided to have extra food.” It feels more like once I start picking at it late at night, something kind of shifts.\n\nI’ll tell myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago. Put it away. You’re done.” I can actually hear myself thinking that. But then my hand is still going back to the container, or I’m opening the fridge again, or I’m taking just one more bite of something. And then “one more” turns into more than one more.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, because I’m a grown man, you know? I make decisions all day at work, I handle things, I’m responsible. But in those moments it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore, even though part of me is aware I should stop. Like I’m watching myself keep going and I’m frustrated with myself while I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes cause you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes and no, I guess. It’s not like I’ve stopped functioning, if that’s what you mean. I still get up and go to my shipping and receiving shifts, I’m still doing my job, getting things checked in, keeping track of inventory, all of that. I’m not missing work over it or anything like that.\n\nBut it does get in my head more than I’d like to admit. At home, especially later in the evening, I’ve started kind of avoiding being in the kitchen with everyone sometimes. Like if my family is hanging around after dinner, talking or putting food away, I’ll make an excuse to go sit somewhere else or go upstairs, because I feel tense around the food. I worry somebody’s going to notice I’m acting weird, or that I’m too focused on what’s left over.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because it sounds small when I say it out loud, but it makes me uncomfortable. I don’t want my family watching me and thinking, “Why is he hovering around the fridge?” or “Why does he look nervous?” So I guess it has caused problems in that way. I’m still handling my responsibilities, but I’m definitely not relaxed about it, and it’s making me pull back from normal family time a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or weighing myself regularly, so I’d be guessing if I gave you a number.\n\nMy clothes… I don’t think there’s been some huge change where I suddenly can’t button my pants or anything like that. Maybe some days I feel heavier or bloated, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just how I’m feeling after those nights. I’ve sort of avoided checking, too, because I don’t really want to see it if it has changed.\n\nSo… significant changes? Not that I can clearly point to. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel bad about yourself after these late-night eating episodes, even if it's not exactly guilt or shame?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say always. I mean… I don’t feel good after, definitely. There’s usually this heaviness, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t know if it’s always the same feeling or if I’d call it bad about myself every single time.\n\nSometimes it’s more frustration than shame. Like I’m irritated with myself, almost like I broke a promise to myself, even if I never said it out loud. Other times I just feel kind of flat afterward, like tired and annoyed and wanting to not think about it. And then there are nights where I’ll try to tell myself, “It’s not that big a deal, just go to bed,” because I don’t want to sit there beating myself up.\n\nSo… yeah, it affects how I feel afterward, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s always this clear guilt or shame thing every time. It’s more like I don’t feel proud of it, and I’d rather it hadn’t happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these late-night episodes, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. Because I’ll go into it thinking I’m just going to have a little bit, or I’ll just pick at something while I’m putting it away, and then I’ll tell myself, like, “Okay, that’s enough, Santiago. Stop now. You’re done.”\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back. It’s not like I black out or anything, I know what I’m doing, but it feels like the decision part of me isn’t really in charge anymore. I’ll be standing there telling myself to close the container or walk away from the fridge, and instead I’m taking another bite, then another one.\n\nThat’s what worries me, because in my head I do want to stop. I’m not enjoying it the whole time. There’s this moment where I know I’ve had enough, and I still keep going anyway, like I don’t get to make the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have these late-night eating episodes been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than three months. I’ve been thinking about that, actually, because it didn’t just start last week or anything.\n\nI’d say it’s been around… maybe about ten months now? It kind of lines up with when my aunt moved into the apartment. After that there just always seemed to be extra food around—leftovers, snacks, things people brought over or she’d buy—and I think that’s when these late-night episodes started becoming more of a regular thing.\n\nAt first I didn’t think of it as anything, you know, it was just like, “Oh, there’s food there, I’ll have some,” but looking back, yeah… it’s been going on most of this past year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I know what I’m eating, so it’s not like I’m confused or anything, but control is the part that feels missing.\n\nWhat worries me is that once I start picking at food late at night, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago, stop now,” and I really do mean it in that moment. Like I can hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just keeps going back, like I don’t get to make the decision anymore. I’ll think, “Close it, put it away, go to bed,” and instead I’m taking another bite.\n\nSo… no, I don’t feel fully in control. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though part of me is trying to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t really eat it the way I would if I was sitting down at the table with my family or having a normal meal. It’s faster than that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying to get through it before anyone hears me or wakes up, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be standing there in the kitchen, or by the fridge, and I’m eating quickly, almost rushing it. Sometimes I barely even taste it. I’ll realize afterward that I didn’t really enjoy it the way I thought I was going to—I was just sort of getting it down.\n\nAnd it feels embarrassing to say, but yeah, there’s this hurry to it. Not like I’m starving and savoring food, more like I’m trying to finish before I get caught or before I have to think too much about what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes where you feel like you can't stop ever happen during the day, or is it always at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because the ones I really notice—the ones where it feels like I can’t stop—are mostly the late-night ones.\n\nDuring the day, I mean… I eat, obviously, and sometimes I might snack more than I planned or grab something without really thinking about it, especially if I’m busy or stressed, but I don’t know if it has that same feeling. The night ones stand out more because it’s quiet and I’m by myself and then I realize I’ve kept going past the point where I meant to stop.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say it never happens during the day, because maybe I’m not paying attention the same way, but when I think about the episodes that bother me and feel out of control, those are really at night.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. Not in a big dramatic way, like I’m missing work or not showing up for people. I still get to my shipping and receiving shifts, I’m doing what I’m supposed to do there, and I’m still handling my responsibilities at home as far as I can tell.\n\nBut with family… I’ve noticed I kind of avoid being around the kitchen later in the evening if other people are there. Like if someone’s hanging around after dinner, talking or cleaning up or just sitting there, I’ll make an excuse to go somewhere else. It’s not that I don’t want to be with them exactly, it’s more that I feel tense around the food and I’m worried they’ll notice I’m acting weird.\n\nI don’t think most of them realize that’s what’s going on. They might just think I’m tired or being quiet. But I know I’m pulling back a little, especially late at night, because I don’t want anyone seeing how uneasy I get. It makes me feel embarrassed, honestly, because it’s my own family and I’m still trying to hide that part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say always. I mean, a lot of the time there’s some kind of bad feeling afterward, but I’m trying to think if it’s guilt exactly every single time.\n\nSometimes it’s more like embarrassment, like, “Why did I do that again?” Or I’ll feel kind of disappointed in myself, especially if I was telling myself earlier in the evening that I wasn’t going to do it. Other times I might just feel uncomfortable and want to put it out of my head as fast as possible, like clean up whatever wrappers or dishes are there and go to bed and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… yeah, there’s usually at least a moment where I don’t feel good about it. But I’m not sure I can honestly say it’s the same strong guilt or shame every time. Sometimes it’s just this quick sinking feeling, and then I try not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes significantly impacted your daily life or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I guess “significantly” is hard for me to judge. I mean, I’m still getting up and going to my shipping and receiving shifts. I’m not calling out because of it or anything like that, and I’m still doing what I need to do around the house. So from the outside, I don’t know that someone would look at my life and say it’s falling apart.\n\nBut it does bother me more than I’d like to admit. It’s in my head, especially later in the day, and I’ve noticed I kind of change what I do because of it. Like after dinner, if family is still around the kitchen, I’ll avoid hanging around there sometimes. I’ll make some excuse or go off somewhere else, not because I don’t want to be with them, but because I feel uneasy around the food and I’m worried somebody’s going to notice I’m acting strange.\n\nSo… it hasn’t wrecked my daily life, exactly. I’m still functioning. But yeah, it causes me distress, and it’s starting to make me pull back from certain moments with family, especially at night. That part doesn’t feel good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel an overwhelming urge to continue, rather than just eating because you're hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that worries me the most, honestly. Because it doesn’t feel like normal hunger by that point. It’s usually late at night, and I’ll start just picking at something, telling myself it’s no big deal, just a little bit.\n\nBut then there’s this point where I know I should stop. Like I’ll actually say to myself in my head, “That’s enough, Santiago.” Sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself, you know? And I mean it when I say it. I’m not sitting there thinking, “Oh, I don’t care.” I do care.\n\nBut then my hand goes back again. And again. And it feels like the decision already got taken out of my hands somehow, even though I’m the one doing it. That’s the strange part. I’m not starving in those moments. It’s more like once I’ve started, there’s this pull to keep going, and stopping feels harder than it should. I can tell myself to stop, but my body just… keeps reaching.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t really eat it the way I would if I was sitting down at the table with my family or having a normal meal. It’s faster than that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying to get through it before anyone hears me or wakes up, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be standing there in the kitchen, or by the fridge, and I’m eating quickly, almost rushing it. Sometimes I barely even taste it. I’ll realize afterward that I didn’t really enjoy it the way I thought I was going to—I was just sort of getting it down.\n\nAnd it feels embarrassing to say, but yeah, there’s this hurry to it. Not like I’m starving and savoring food, more like I’m trying to finish before I get caught or before I have to think too much about what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes where you feel like you can't stop ever happen during the day, or is it always at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because the ones I really notice—the ones where it feels like I can’t stop—are mostly the late-night ones.\n\nDuring the day, I mean… I eat, obviously, and sometimes I might snack more than I planned or grab something without really thinking about it, especially if I’m busy or stressed, but I don’t know if it has that same feeling. The night ones stand out more because it’s quiet and I’m by myself and then I realize I’ve kept going past the point where I meant to stop.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say it never happens during the day, because maybe I’m not paying attention the same way, but when I think about the episodes that bother me and feel out of control, those are really at night.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. Not in a big dramatic way, like I’m missing work or not showing up for people. I still get to my shipping and receiving shifts, I’m doing what I’m supposed to do there, and I’m still handling my responsibilities at home as far as I can tell.\n\nBut with family… I’ve noticed I kind of avoid being around the kitchen later in the evening if other people are there. Like if someone’s hanging around after dinner, talking or cleaning up or just sitting there, I’ll make an excuse to go somewhere else. It’s not that I don’t want to be with them exactly, it’s more that I feel tense around the food and I’m worried they’ll notice I’m acting weird.\n\nI don’t think most of them realize that’s what’s going on. They might just think I’m tired or being quiet. But I know I’m pulling back a little, especially late at night, because I don’t want anyone seeing how uneasy I get. It makes me feel embarrassed, honestly, because it’s my own family and I’m still trying to hide that part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say always. I mean, a lot of the time there’s some kind of bad feeling afterward, but I’m trying to think if it’s guilt exactly every single time.\n\nSometimes it’s more like embarrassment, like, “Why did I do that again?” Or I’ll feel kind of disappointed in myself, especially if I was telling myself earlier in the evening that I wasn’t going to do it. Other times I might just feel uncomfortable and want to put it out of my head as fast as possible, like clean up whatever wrappers or dishes are there and go to bed and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… yeah, there’s usually at least a moment where I don’t feel good about it. But I’m not sure I can honestly say it’s the same strong guilt or shame every time. Sometimes it’s just this quick sinking feeling, and then I try not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes significantly impacted your daily life or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I guess “significantly” is hard for me to judge. I mean, I’m still getting up and going to my shipping and receiving shifts. I’m not calling out because of it or anything like that, and I’m still doing what I need to do around the house. So from the outside, I don’t know that someone would look at my life and say it’s falling apart.\n\nBut it does bother me more than I’d like to admit. It’s in my head, especially later in the day, and I’ve noticed I kind of change what I do because of it. Like after dinner, if family is still around the kitchen, I’ll avoid hanging around there sometimes. I’ll make some excuse or go off somewhere else, not because I don’t want to be with them, but because I feel uneasy around the food and I’m worried somebody’s going to notice I’m acting strange.\n\nSo… it hasn’t wrecked my daily life, exactly. I’m still functioning. But yeah, it causes me distress, and it’s starting to make me pull back from certain moments with family, especially at night. That part doesn’t feel good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel an overwhelming urge to continue, rather than just eating because you're hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that worries me the most, honestly. Because it doesn’t feel like normal hunger by that point. It’s usually late at night, and I’ll start just picking at something, telling myself it’s no big deal, just a little bit.\n\nBut then there’s this point where I know I should stop. Like I’ll actually say to myself in my head, “That’s enough, Santiago.” Sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself, you know? And I mean it when I say it. I’m not sitting there thinking, “Oh, I don’t care.” I do care.\n\nBut then my hand goes back again. And again. And it feels like the decision already got taken out of my hands somehow, even though I’m the one doing it. That’s the strange part. I’m not starving in those moments. It’s more like once I’ve started, there’s this pull to keep going, and stopping feels harder than it should. I can tell myself to stop, but my body just… keeps reaching."
  }
